#2898. Amazon's Astro home robot has fans but looks destined for the 'trash heap.' Why it's trying again

Jeff Bezos made a bold bet in 2017. A sci-fi and robotics enthusiast, Bezos was convinced that consumers would have a robot in their home in the not-too-distant future.

The Amazon founder and then-CEO told a group of the company's top device executives to get to work on building one, and within a few years, the team came up with the $1,450 Astro.

The public got its first look at Astro, essentially an Alexa-equipped tablet on wheels that can autonomously navigate a home, recognize faces and act as a roving security monitor, when it rolled on stage at Amazon's fall 2021 event.

But it never evolved beyond an invite-only product. Astro shipped to just a few thousand customers, some of whom now believe the robot's days are numbered. The company also released an Astro for businesses in 2023, before sunsetting it less than a year later.

Amazon isn't giving up on Bezos' dream of a household robot just yet, despite its stumbles with Astro.

Over the past several months, the company has assembled a new team that's working on a "new-to-world robot product," according to a CNBC analysis of job posts.

"Robots are about to step out of the movies and into our homes," a recent job listing for an engineer, which has since been removed, stated. The post details a product capable of navigating "dynamic home environments" and "autonomous operation alongside family members."

Amazon is known for making long-term bets at the risk of "being misunderstood" in the short term if it gives the company an early advantage in new markets, like it did with the Alexa voice assistant or Kindle e-reader.

CEO Andy Jassy reminded investors of this approach in his latest annual letter, where he said artificial intelligence and robotics were among the "biggest inflections of our lifetime."

"We try to anticipate what will make customers' lives easier and better every day, and invent the next inflection," Jassy wrote.

Analysts also expect the humanoid market to surge in the coming years. Barclays forecast in a January report that the global market, which it currently values at $2 billion to $3 billion, could reach roughly $40 billion by 2035, or as high as $200 billion "under the most optimistic scenarios."

Earlier this year, Amazon redeployed much of the team behind Astro and brought in an MIT roboticist to work on the home robot project, LinkedIn profiles show.

It also made a splashy acquisition to supercharge the effort.

In March, Amazon scooped up New York-based Fauna Robotics, which has built a 3.5-foot-tall humanoid called Sprout. At the time, the company said Fauna would become part of a new division dubbed the Personal Robotics Group.

Some Personal Robotics Group staffers previously worked in Amazon's Lab126 R&D unit, which has been behind many of Amazon's most successful consumer hardware products, like the Kindle e-reader, Echo smart speakers, Fire TV and Fire Tablet. Lab126 was also behind one of Amazon's biggest flops, the Fire Phone.

It's unclear what kind of home robot the group is developing, or the types of tasks it could handle, but a June job posting hinted at an effort to "create robots that are not just functional, but delightful."

Amazon could be looking to adapt Fauna's robots for homes or businesses. The Sprout humanoid, which was designed to be "friendly and capable" with expressive yellow eyebrows, has primarily been used by developers, as well as commercial and academic researchers.

An Amazon job posting published online in October describes a "Field Support Engineer" who can assist with customer support and on-site repair, while another listing seeks a user experience designer to "define what it feels like to live with a Fauna robot."

"Our goal is simple: make robots people actually want to live and interact with in everyday human spaces," the listing states.

Amazon spokesperson Brad Glasser told CNBC in a statement that the company acquired Fauna because it's "committed to tackling the challenges that can unlock the personal robotics category."

"We're optimistic about a future where personal robots are helping people in meaningful ways in everyday life, and we intend to play an important role in making that future a reality," Glasser said.

Glasser noted that there remains a substantial gap between what's possible in a controlled lab and what's possible in settings like the living room or a lobby. Amazon is working with the robotics community to solve these challenges, he said.

Emily Lugo Velez has been living with the Astro in her North Carolina home since 2023. Lugo Velez, who worked in IT and now runs an AI advisory business, said she and her husband weren't fazed by the robot's $1,000-plus price tag and quickly joined the waitlist.

"We spent so much money on this thing," Lugo Velez, 52, said in an interview. "I feel like we're just stuck with it, and we'll let it run around the house until it eventually dies, then send it to the trash heap."

She said she still uses the device to check on her pets and appliances when she's out of the house. But beyond that, Astro has primarily become a party trick and a "trip hazard."

Most days, she tells Astro to go back to its charging dock.

Executives who worked on Astro had discussed making it more capable by attaching an arm to pick up and inspect items without breaking them, said Ken Washington, who served as Amazon's VP of consumer robotics until 2023.

He told CNBC that he pushed for a bipedal robot, but leadership disagreed.

"We had Jeff [Bezos] in for show and tells and reviews, and I watched his body language pretty closely," Washington said. "I think he was disappointed with the lack of functionality, but I think he was impressed with its ability to map and navigate an undefined space under all lighting conditions."

For all the struggles with Astro, Amazon has a rich history in robotics.

The company acquired Kiva Systems for $775 million in 2012, which introduced mobile robots to its vast fulfillment network. Since then, Amazon's warehouse automation efforts have ballooned to over one million robots across its facilities that can pick, sort and ferry items to workers.

But homes offer unique obstacles for robots to navigate.

"The home is a completely different space," said Jeff Burnstein, president of the trade group Association for Advancing Automation, who has been in the robotics industry for 45 years. "Children, pets, grandparents, stairways. Nobody's home is set up exactly the same, so it's a much more challenging environment for a robot, certainly a robot that's walking."

Amazon may have more luck its second time around because the tools for building robots have advanced by "leaps and bounds" over the past few years, said Colin Angle, a co-founder and former CEO of Roomba maker iRobot.

Amazon attempted to acquire iRobot in 2022 before walking away from the deal two years later amid growing regulatory pushback.

"The toolkit that the Astro team had when they did it was clubs with nails in them, just incredibly primitive," Angle said. "It's difficult to understate just how much the robot industry has changed in the last two years."

Advances in AI models, sensors, battery systems and key mechanical components like actuators have spurred a new wave of development around robots for industrial and consumer use.

Elon Musk's Tesla has been developing its bipedal Optimus robots since 2021. Startups including Apptronik, Boston Dynamics, China's Unitree, Figure and 1X are also building humanoids. Agility Robotics, the company behind the bipedal Digit robot that counts Amazon as one of its backers, went public via a SPAC on Thursday.

Venture capital firms poured $33.4 billion into robotics and physical AI companies in the first half of the year, compared with $28.7 billion for all of 2025, according to PitchBook data.

Most humanoids are being developed with the manufacturing, logistics and healthcare industries in mind, but a growing group of companies are attempting to build robots for the home.

Apple is reportedly working on several home robot projects, including a tabletop device that resembles the Pixar lamp. Hugging Face, which Nvidia agreed to acquire for almost $13 billion in September, released the $399 Microduck in August. Angle, in May, launched a startup building fuzzy companion robots called Familiar Machines & Magic. Companies including Sunday, Tau and Nori claim their robots can help with household chores like folding laundry and clearing the table, but they remain mostly prototype or niche devices.

"[Astro] was so ahead of its time," Washington said. "It was such a big, massive leap that I felt like we had the right to own the home."

The robot's limited functionality and its $1,000-plus price tag likely made it a tough sell for many consumers, said a former Amazon employee who worked on Astro, who asked not to be named in order to speak frankly about the project.

"Our poor little Astro was around $2,000 when it came out, and that's a lot," the ex-employee said. "That's a lot for a consumer, and they want a robot that does a lot."

Amazon takes a long term view on new markets like personal robotics, and values what can be gained by being an early entrant, Glasser said.

Astro was designed to help Amazon build competencies and focus its ambition, he added. The company has applied many of the learnings, talent and experience from Astro into its other robotics efforts, Glasser said.

In Facebook groups for Astro owners, users have complained of diminished customer service and griped that Amazon's AIexa+ AI assistant isn't supported on Astro.

Some users have interpreted this to mean Astro could be discontinued. Others have tried to dump their units at a discount on eBay and Facebook Marketplace.

Glasser said Amazon continues to support Astro customers.

Joe Martin, an Astro owner who lives near Tucson, Arizona, said he feels he's gotten his money's worth out of the device over the past four years. He'd welcome another robot into his home if it could do more, like helping out around the house.

"Astro was $1,000 and it was pretty limited, but if it could bring a drink to me or dust furniture, I'd be intrigued by that," Martin, 65, said. "I think that's right around the corner."

#2897. Lions snap counts: Malcolm Rodriguez, Rock Ya-Sin step up vs. Dolphins

In Week 9, the Detroit Lions moved to 6-2 after beating the Dolphins in Miami. The Lions had to finesse their roster for this game, as they managed several player injuries from Week 8 and a surprise benching along the starting offensive line.

Let's look at the snap counts from this game and see what they mean for the depth chart hierarchy and what we can ascertain moving forward.

It wasn't Goff's cleanest game, but he got the job done when they needed him to, including executing a quarterback sneak to seal the game. With this win, Goff ties Hall of Fame QB Bobby Layne for the second-most wins (54) in Lions history, trailing only Matthew Stafford (74).

Sione Vaki: 19 (32%) — special teams snaps 15 (62%)

Gibbs touched the ball on half his offensive snaps, but the Lions appeared to scale back his touches a bit in this game and gave Vaki a few more opportunities. Mahogany has clearly been the better player, so let's hope the team can move forward with him back in the starting lineup.

3T Skyler Gill-Howard: 28 (49%) — 4 (17%) — 1 offensive snap (2%)

The interior defensive line has settled into a groove and are splitting their snaps in a very similar way week to week. Gill-Howard (who continues to get some usage as a fullback on offense in goal-line sets) and Williams are rotating through snaps opposite McNeill, with Onwuzurike supplementing the group. The group is finding a lot of success with this split, so there's no reason to change it.

Moore, like Wonnum, split snaps opposite Hutchinson with Lacy, and Lucas—a practice squad elevation who saw his first snaps of NFL action, registering one pressure on nine pass-rushing reps, per PFF, but surprisingly saw no special-teams snaps.

WILL/MIKE Malcolm Rodriguez: 55 (96%) — 4 (17%)

WILL/SAM Trevor Nowaske: 30 (53%) — 19 (79%)

WILL Amen Ogbongbemiga: 0 (0%) — 15 (62%)

With Jack Campbell on injured reserve, Rodriguez was promoted to a starting role alongside Barnes (who now wears the green dot play-call relayer helmet), and the pair split linebacking roles throughout the game. Nowaske continued to operate in his LB3 role, while Bachie, Ogbonbemiga, and Britt only played on special teams.

With Reed out due to a hip injury, Ya-Sin returned to the starting lineup, slotting in opposite McCreary. Rakestraw found the field on the outside in subpackage sets, while Dorsey continues to see situational work. Whiteside, who was just signed off the practice squad over the weekend, got some reps late in the game.

Since Branch returned to his starting role, the Lions have been rotating Clark and Abney at the opposite safety spot. However, the coaches love Clark's veteran savvy and increased his playing time considerably in this game, and he rewarded them with an interception. Abney did find the field in three-safety sets, but that formation was only used sparingly.

#2895. Could supplements be the next big opportunity in the GLP-1 weight-loss drug boom?

Supplements could be the next major sector to benefit from the weight-loss drug boom, Novonesis CEO told CNBC on Monday, as the biosolutions company explores whether microbiome products can help patients on popular GLP-1 medicines.

"We have scientific evidence that [is] going to support the patients [on] GLP-1s feeling better, as a companion during the treatment," CEO Ester Baiget told "Squawk Box Europe," referencing an ongoing study with Novo Nordisk.

GLP-1 medications and dietary supplements, such as those being developed by Novonesis, could play complementary roles in managing obesity and supporting longer-term health, she argued.

While medications can help treat obesity, improving the gut microbiome through nutrition and scientifically backed supplements could support longer-term health, Baiget said.

The microbiome refers to the community of microorganisms, including bacteria, fungi and viruses, that live in or on different parts of the body, particularly in the gut.

Baiget also sees strong commercial demand for supplements with proven health benefits.

"We see that when we prove science with answers, there is a tremendous pull in the market," she said. Novonesis is currently studying what Baiget referred to as "a unique cocktail" of pro-, pre- and post-biotics to support patients taking GLP-1s made by Wegovy and Ozempic maker Novo Nordisk.

The lucrative weight-loss drugs market pioneered by Novo Nordisk and U.S. rival Eli Lilly has had far-reaching consequences beyond health companies.

From consumer staples and food companies reassessing meal composition and sizes, to fashion companies seeing a boost from consumers buying new clothes after dropping sizes, drugs like Wegovy and Zepbound are widely considered to be reshaping consumer spending across industries.

According to a PwC survey of U.K. adults, about 70% of GLP-1 users are spending less on snacks, while 40% are spending more on vitamins and supplements. More than 80% of respondents who had now stopped taking the medicine said they maintained some of the dietary changes they adopted while on treatment.

Meanwhile, the global dietary supplements market was valued at an estimated $209.5 billion in 2025 and is projected to more than double to $431.7 billion by 2033, according to Grand View Research.

Growing health awareness is driving demand for supplements and functional foods and drinks containing ingredients such as vitamins and probiotics, as consumers seek benefits beyond basic nutrition, the market research firm found.

The global probiotic supplements market was estimated to be worth around $10.5 billion in 2025, growing to $17.5 billion by 2033.

Some research has linked GLP-1 treatment to insufficient protein intake and deficiencies in certain vitamins and minerals, but the extent to which the medications themselves are responsible remains unclear. The microbiome-based products being studied by Novonesis are different from protein and vitamin supplements, and any potential benefit for GLP-1 users has yet to be established.

For Novonesis, if benefits are scientifically proven, it could be a welcome boost to its human health business, which is currently under pressure.

Its human health business reported an 8% decline in organic sales in the third quarter, weighed down by weaker demand for probiotic supplements sold through offline channels in North America.

The company, formed through the merger of Novozymes and Chr. Hansen in 2024, is a leader in enzymes and microbial solutions used across industries such as food, agriculture, and healthcare. Its products are used in everything from dairy fermentation to dietary supplements.

The company holds nearly 50% of the enzymes and cultures market, compared with around 20% for rival International Flavors & Fragrances, according to Morningstar.

Novonesis gets about a third of total sales from the North American market, while Human Health accounts for about 11% of its total sales.

#2894. Why tech workers are flocking to NYC

Erin Sullivan had only visited New York City once before she decided to take a job in the Big Apple.

But that visit made a strong impression: "Having the freedom of just hopping on the subway and getting anywhere was so magical for me," she says. "I knew that I wanted to end back up" in the city.

After graduating from the University of Oklahoma in 2023 with a computer science degree, Sullivan, 25, landed a role as a software engineer at Bank of America in New York City.

She's part of a growing contingent of tech workers who are drawn to the city.

The New York metro area is now home to the most tech workers in the country with 394,300 jobs, edging out the San Francisco Bay Area's 375,730 jobs, according to an August report from CBRE, a real estate services and investment firm.

Between 2022 and 2025, New York's pool of tech employees grew by over 30,000, while San Francisco's shrank by nearly 24,000 workers.

In today's tech talent market, "the Big Apple is on top," says Lauren Crowley Corrinet, vice chairman at CBRE.

But a big part of New York's tech hiring boom comes from outside the traditional tech sector, according to Julie Samuels, president and CEO of Tech:NYC, a nonprofit representing the city's technology industry.

According to Tech:NYC's data, just 35% of NYC's tech employees work at tech companies and startups, while 64% are employed by non-tech companies like banks and media organizations, based on their report published in November 2026.

"When you think about industries like finance, like real estate — industries that have been in New York for a really long time — it has become increasingly clear that technology is driving their work as well," Samuels says.

To keep up with artificial intelligence developments, "everyone is hiring" tech workers and consultants, she says: "All these traditional industries are thinking about how they can bring AI into the fold in their own work."

And AI companies are expanding their reach in the city, too: OpenAI leased a 90,000-square-foot office in lower Manhattan, and Anthropic says it plans to double its New York workforce to 1,000 employees in 2026.

It's no mystery why workers are drawn to New York City, according to Corrinet: "Everything's here, and it's just such a rich and engaging place for people to live."

But New York's high cost of living can pose a challenge even for tech workers "who make six figures right out of college," according to Samuels. On the other hand, there are valid concerns that an influx of highly paid tech workers could worsen the affordability crisis for other New Yorkers, she says.

Her goal is to ensure that New York's growing tech presence benefits all New Yorkers, not just those who work in the industry.

Watch the video above to hear more from Samuels, Corrinet and Sullivan — plus more tech workers who are choosing NYC.

#2893. Nets get run over by Flagg, Irving, and Mavericks, losing 130-93

This time, the Net took a far more direct route to a loss.

Continuing their first legitimate road trip of the season, Brooklyn sure looked like weary travelers tonight in Dallas. They also played this one as the second half of a back-to-back, and that likely didn't help either.

In that prior game, the Nets let a contest they had complete command over slip away in its final moments. Rookie Mikel Brown Jr. kept his team in the driver's seat for as long as he could, dropping 13 points in the fourth quarter.

Held out for back injury management, the Nets were without him tonight, and any sense of control over whatsoever. It was in a lot of ways the worst loss of the season and that's saying something. Br00klyn is now 2-8.

Danny Wolf got the start once again for Egor Dëmin, who's also still out with a bone bruise in his right leg. But even with a seven-footer technically in their backcourt, Dallas looked like the bigger team early on. In the game's first few minutes, the Nets missed an abundance of attempts around the rim and even saw three of their shots get rejected. The Mavs nearly doubled Brooklyn up on the glass as well, out-rebounding them in the first 13-7. However, they did triple them in paint scoring, adding 18 points inside to Brooklyn's six.

Those statistical discrepancies would only swell as the game went on. By the end of it, the Nets were out-rebounded 46-30 and lost in the paint 64-40. The score was ugly too, but I'll save that score for later.

Keon Ellis also returned to the rotation in the first after not logging any minutes yesterday vs Atlanta. Still expected to be a spark plug kind of player for the Nets this year, Ellis' reappearance didn't give the Nets much juice, but nobody else outside of Michael Porter Jr. could either.

Brooklyn began the game 4-of-14 from the field. Kyrie Irving alone had the same amount of field goals as them through the 3:21 mark of the first, adding nine points on 4-of-7 shooting in the opening period. But after Porter Jr. got free in transition for a few triples, everything else started to fall for him. He scored 11 points in the period's final 2:51 and 15 in the quarter, just beating out Cooper Flagg, who had 14. That kept us out of blowout territory, with the Nets down 33-26 entering the second.

No, that's what the second quarter was for. The Mavericks started it with an extended 21-6 run in the period's first five minutes, quickly hurling the Nets into a 22-point hole. After a turnaround jumper from Kyrie with 4:06 to go in the quarter, it got as deep as 26 points.

"We were not connected, and we made a lot of mistakes," Fernández said. "And then a team like this, when they're in a rhythm with the players that they have, it's when you can get hurt. And I think they made some tough ones, and we also allowed some."

Were than any positives? Nolan Traoré continued to look like a confident, true point guard, navigating around the Dallas defense, directing traffic, and picking the right shooting spots. Turnovers, which were a major problem in the fumbled game vs the Hawks, also weren't as abundant. The Nets even won that battle in the first half, forcing Dallas into 12 and only committing eight themselves.

Still, Brooklyn posted .385/.287 splits going into the break, while the Mavericks shot .619/.400, and no silver linings or improvements around the margins could cover that up. The Mavs led at half 66-46. By that point, Flagg had 20 points, seven assists, and four rebounds on just 10 shots. Brooklyn's only man in double figures was Porter Jr., who had 16 points with seven boards on 3-of-7 downtown shooting.

If you expected the Nets to take that first-half embarrassment personally and respond accordingly, you were mistaken. By the 4:20 mark of the third, Dallas had gotten up by 30.

Things got borderline humiliating when Noah Clowney fouled out of the game with 2:23 to go in the third after shoving Morez Johnson Jr. in the chest away from the ball. They got into it earlier in the quarter too, where officials tagged Johnson Jr. with a technical foul after he knocked down Clowney on a box out. This time, however, Clowney took the guilty charge. He picked up the six fouls to disqualify himself in just eight minutes or burn.

"That's something that he's got to figure out," Fernández said. "He put us on the bonus right away and in one minute, and that's tough. And I think that it comes from a good place because I know he's extremely competitive guy. But then doing the war on your own, it's just tough at times.

"You got to choose the right way to do it, and channel all that energy and sometimes frustration the right way. I think the one person that you know is helping him and will help him is Juwan [Howard] because he's been there. I know that the intentions are great. It's just, keep doing what's best for the group. A nd we need Noah Clowney. L ike that first half against Charlotte, we don't win that game without him, so we need the best version of himself because we know how good he is."

And, at the same time, the Mavericks kept inflating the lead, just having fun out there. With two seconds left to go in the third period, Naji Marshal connected with Kyrie with a beautiful cross-court dime to set him up and-one…

Dallas also opened the fourth with a Cooper Flagg three after Wolf went under a screen for him, which extended their lead to a game-high 32 points. Flagg might've had a career night had the Nets been more competitive and gave Dusty May any reason to keep him in, but the star sophomore didn't play past the 8:28 mark of the fourth. He still finished with 25 points, five rebounds, and a career-high 12 assists while shooting 8-of-13 from the field and 3-of-5 from deep. Irving finished with 21 points, four assists, and three rebounds while shooting 9-15 from the field and 2-7 from three.

Around that point, Drake Powell and Josh Minott came in, giving us something fresh to watch, even if necessarily not fun.

Minott did hit two "Chris-Paul-hits-a-huge-three-to cut-the-lead-to-down…" shots on back-to-back possessions on near the quarter's midpoint. Leveraging the defense's newfound awareness of his shooting, he attacked a closeout and got up for a jam a few minutes later as well. He finished the game with 10 points, two steals, and two rebounds while shooting 4-of-10 from the field and 2-of-8 from deep.

Powell got through the defense for a rushed, but made floater that came back down and in after bouncing high off the back iron, but produced little outside of that.

With Brooklyn still down 25+, Dallas gave Jett Howard, son of Nets assistant coach Juwan Howard, some run as well. In front of his pops, he added five points and grabbed a rebound in three minutes of play.

That was cool. Everything else that happened on the court tonight was not. The Nets ended up losing by a whopping 37 points, where nobody on the team put up more than 20 points. Porter Jr. got up to 19 with nine rebounds while shooting 9-of-19 from the field and 4-of-11 from three. Randle contributed 13 points, four assists and four rebounds while shooting 5-of-10 from the field. He also had three turnovers and was a -33. Traoré got to 14 points and two dimes, shooting 6-15 from the field and 2-of-6 from deep.

Final: Dallas Mavericks 130, Brooklyn Nets 93

Brooklyn's road trip continues with a Wednesday evening game in New Orleans vs the Pelicans. The Pels are 4-5 at the time of writing. The Nets split their series against them last year. This one tips off at 8:00 p.m. EST.

#2892. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2890. Rubio sees little hope of diplomatic breakthrough to Mideast deadlock amid 'outrageous' Iranian demands

U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio has accused Iran of "outrageous and ridiculous" demands as efforts to reach a lasting resolution in the Middle East war appeared stalled.

Speaking with reporters in Florida on Sunday evening before traveling to Paris for the G7 foreign ministers' meeting, Rubio indicated the two sides remain far apart on negotiations, after Tehran proposed shortening the timetable for restarting talks from seven days to two.

Rubio said President Donald Trump would prefer to solve "this and every problem diplomatically," but added that "sometimes that's not possible, unfortunately."

Energy prices moved lower on Monday morning after President Trump said Sunday that further strikes were "highly unlikely" if negotiations progressed toward a strong agreement.

Brent crude, the international price benchmark, was down 0.75% at $97.58 per barrel by 5:25 a.m. ET. Meanwhile, U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures for December delivery were trading 0.42% lower at $90.45.

Trump said in a CNN interview prior to Rubio's comments that a resumption of hostilities in the more than eight-month-long conflict was unlikely, provided that talks to constrain Iran's nuclear ambitions and reopen the Strait of Hormuz can progress.

"I wouldn't say we're on the verge of a breakthrough," Rubio said. "Right now, it hasn't been very productive because the Iranian demands are simply not just unacceptable but, in some cases, outrageous and ridiculous and really not realistic."

Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesperson Esmaeil Baghaei said Monday that Tehran wants U.S. sanctions lifted and the war ended on all fronts before negotiations begin.

#2888. Jordi Fernandez reveals what Nets hope first-round picks gain from time in G League

Jordi Fernandez reveals what Nets hope first-round picks gain from time in G League appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Brooklyn Nets made an NBA-record seven first-round picks in the last two drafts. Several of those players find themselves outside of the team's rotation as it sheds the tanking label this season. Drake Powell, Ben Saraf and Joshua Jefferson each played for Brooklyn's G League affiliate, the Long Island Nets, during the opening games of the G League season this weekend.

What do the Nets hope those players gain from that experience?

"We don't say down in the G League because we believe that the club is the same," said Brooklyn head coach Jordi Fernandez. "So in Long Island, with our coaching staff and our players there, the number one thing is to compete and play extremely hard. And then, as we've shared before, we have individual development plans for all the players, and their goals and the way we want them to play are the same there as they are here. It's just the context is different. The minutes are different. The teammates are different. The shot may be different, but who they need to be as players is pretty similar. So I'll give you an example. If you need to be a second side pick-and-roll guy, play off two dribbles, catch and shoot and defend and pick up full court, I don't want you to be playing in Long Island and taking 35 shots and playing ISO game.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Just a simple way to describe it. So I think communication is extremely important, and I think that the way it works here, from what I've seen, is excellent. And I think those guys are excited. Being exposed to real games and practices is very, very, very important, and everybody's got different paths to develop. Sitting on a bench is never good."

Powell, Saraf, Danny Wolf and Nolan Traore each spent extended time in the G League as rookies last season.

Powell and Saraf have been behind Traore, Egor Demin, Mikel Brown, Keon Ellis and Terance Mann in the Nets' backcourt rotation. Meanwhile, Jefferson has been behind Noah Clowney, Danny Wolf and Chaney Johnson in the race for forward minutes with the second unit.

Tyler Bilodeau, the No. 43 pick in the 2026 draft, is also spending time with Long Island to start the G League season. The former UCLA forward has averaged 10.5 points and 3.5 rebounds on 8-of-18 shooting from the field and 5-of-12 from three across his first two appearances with Long Island.

#2887. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.

#2886. 10 thoughts on the Cowboys win over the Colts

The Dallas Cowboys are back in the win column, and all it took was facing an Indianapolis Colts team in despair to pull it off. It wasn't pretty, it was unnecessarily nerve-racking, and it was filled with zany ups and downs.

Regardless, the win gives purpose to the rest of the Cowboys' season, despite still leaving a lot of things to worry about. Here are 10 thoughts on the Cowboys 27-23 win over the Colts on Sunday.

Brian Schottenheimer wanted to show he meant business by committing to the run, using six offensive linemen right out of the gate. Broderick Jones was in on the first play from scrimmage and was used often. The Cowboys had a perfect balance between running and passing with 34 plays of each.

Javonte Williams led the charge with 101 yards rushing, while complementary back Tyler Goodson finished with 77 yards rushing. Both averaged over five yards per carry.

What a strange day for Cowboys receiver CeeDee Lamb. In a game without his pass-catching partner in crime, George Pickens, Lamb did all the heavy lifting in this one. He finished the game with 10 catches for 195 yards.

Weirdly, he was also involved in some odd stuff as he got flagged for two separate offensive pass interference penalties, negating clutch first-down catches. It was a strange mix of good and bad, and sometimes it happened on the same play.

In the fourth quarter, Lamb made a great catch on a crucial third-down play where he wisely got up and started running before being touched. It was great until he eventually fumbled the ball away after a shoestring tackle.

Speaking of penalties, the Cowboys were flagged for just about every type of penalty possible in this game. Three different times they were flagged for offensive pass interference. It felt like every offensive lineman got caught for holding (they didn't).

Even players who weren't playing were getting flagged for stuff, as DeMarvion Overshown was flagged for an unsportsmanlike penalty despite not even playing in the game. Some of the flag stuff was just touchy officiating, but for a team that struggles with discipline, it frustrating to continue to see some of this silliness.

While the offense came through with a nice game-winning score late in the fourth quarter, everything up until that felt underwhelming. The offense did some things, we're not discounting that, but it just felt like they weren't doing enough against the Colts, who are one of the worst defenses in the league.

They continued to have issues near the goal line, as if their playbook starts to shrink. They got it done, but it just feels like Brian Schottenheimer chooses the worst plays possible to do it. Last week, Bryan Anger punted for a season-high four times. On Sunday, he set a new season-high with five punts.

Don't let the win fool you. The Cowboys' offense is starting to show some signs of trouble.

Dak Prescott was a bit off at times, as he was sometimes throwing behind receivers and into coverage. That's not good. Even Prescott's good throws weren't great. If Lamb got tackled in bounds after having to come back on that underthrown ball late in the first half, the Cowboys likely would've had to settle for a field goal there.

Prescott still made some big plays, but he hasn't been as sharp as he usually is, and that will need to change if they are to have a chance against better teams.

In this game, it became more enjoyable to watch the Cowboys' defense play than their offense. At least early on. How did that happen?

The Cowboys' defense played well early in the game, forcing four three-and-outs on the Colts' first five possessions of the game, and Indy's longest play during that span was just seven yards. The Colts only had 86 total yards in the first half.

The Cowboys' defense started to give way some in the second half, but fortunately they were able to come away with big stops in big moments, including two fourth-quarter turnovers. Well done, defense.

Quinnen Williams is so good at football. The Cowboys' stud defensive tackle continues to play incredibly well, as he was constantly living the Colts backfield. He added another three tackles for loss, including a forced fumble that killed an Indy drive in the fourth quarter.

He now has a total of 14 tackles for loss this season, which is a new career-high eclipsing the 12 he had during his All-Pro season in 2022.

While the Cowboys came away with the win, this was another game they tried really hard to give away. The penalties, the turnovers, and even botching a snap that may have caused Brandon Aubrey to miss a field goal he almost always makes.

Again and again, they were doing things that losing teams do. And again, it was against a bad team. They had plenty of chances to pull away, but that is just not their style. They continuously play down to the level of their opponent, as if they just love to live for the drama.

Ryan Flournoy only had two catches on the day, but his second one was a big one. After the Colts went ahead 23-20 late in the fourth quarter, the Cowboys answered back thanks to a big catch by Flournoy.

Prescott took a shot deep and found Flournoy who tracked the ball well even with All-Pro cornerback Sauce Gardner draped all over him. It immediately changed the mood of the game as the Cowboys were instantly in field goal range and now could play for the win.

It was fun to watch the Downs brothers, Caleb and Josh, go against each other, but it was nicer seeing little bro get the better of it. The Cowboys rookie safety had a sack early in the game and then a game-sealing interception late in the fourth quarter. Strangely, it was the Cowboys' first interception of the season by a defensive back.

#2885. Player Grades: Lakers vs. Pistons

One trait JJ Redick teams will have is that they play hard.

Redick had a revelation of sorts early in his coaching career that just trying hard was enough to mask over flaws on most nights in the regular season. From that point on, the Lakers have, for the most part, tried hard.

With previous rosters, playing hard looked different because of a lack of energy or athleticism across the board. On a younger, more athletic, more tenacious team like this, playing hard looks like a team without their superstar jumping all over the Pistons on the road in the first quarter.

It's quite the encouraging trait to have.

So, let's dive into the win. As always, grades are based on expectations for each player. A "B" grade represents the average performance for that player.

28 minutes, 4 points, 2 rebounds, 4 steals, 3 fouls, 2-5 FG, +0

This is Thybulle's third consecutive subpar offensive game. He's hit just one of his last nine threes in the span. Unironically, no longer shooting no-dip threes might have messed with his timing. I don't think his starting spot is in jeopardy yet, but there are plenty of options behind him.

29 minutes, 5 points, 1 rebound, 2 assists, 2 fouls, 1-3 FG, 0-1 3PT, 3-4 FT, +3

An interesting use of LaRavia in this game as he was more of a ballhandler with the Lakers down Luka Dončić. I had hoped that experiment stayed in the 2025-26 season, but it wasn't disastrous on Sunday.

28 minutes, 12 points, 16 rebounds, 4 assists, 1 steal, 3 blocks, 3 fouls, 4 turnovers, 5-10 FG, 2-3 FT, +5

Kessler's ability to stuff the stat sheet is going to make him a sneaky 5×5 candidate. Seven of his 16 rebounds were on the offensive glass and each one felt like it led directly to another basket. A huge showing from him.

31 minutes, 12 points, 1 rebound, 4 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 2 turnovers, 4-12 FG, 2-5 3PT, 2-4 FT +1

Almost all of Grimes' production came in the first quarter, but it was all very useful. He hit four of his first five shots and was a necessary ballhandler to alleviate some of the pressure off Austin Reaves.

Even if he faded as the game went, this was a far more encouraging performance from Grimes than most of his recent outings.

36 minutes, 43 points, 5 rebounds, 4 assists, 3 fouls, 1 turnover, 14-27 FG, 4-6 3PT, 11-11 FT, +21

This is everything you needed from your nearly max player with your superstar out. He took on every bit of Luka's role, dealt with the intense ball pressure all night long and still put up 43 points on great efficiency.

17 minutes, 7 points, 3 rebounds, 2 fouls, 2 turnovers, 3-5 FG, 1-3 3PT, -7

Another relatively quiet night from Mamu as well. He had a couple moments early but also faded as the game went on.

13 minutes, 2 points, 10 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 block, 2 fouls, 2 turnovers, 1-1 FG, 0-1 FT, +2

Vando was immediately impactful in the first quarter, grabbing nearly as many rebounds in that quarter (5) as he had in his last three games (6). His only basket came on a great finish on an and-one as well.

Credit to him for remaining ready even after falling out of the rotation with Adou Thiero's return.

21 minutes, 17 points, 2 rebounds, 1 block, 2 fouls, 6-10 FG, 4-8 3PT, 1-2 FT, +7

For a number of reasons, this was a really strong performance from Ziaire, none more so than his clutch baskets in the fourth quarter. He stepped up and knocked down some huge shots and rightfully closed this game.

24 minutes, 17 points, 2 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 foul, 2 turnovers, 5-10 FG, 0-2 3PT, 7-8 FT, +1

You know what? Fair play to Sexton. It still wasn't a perfect game, especially with him fouling on a 3-pointer at the end. But the Lakers needed him and he had some important buckets throughout the game.

Another brief cameo from Thiero as he works his way back from injury. He still had a highlight with a big dunk after corralling an offensive rebound. It will be interesting to see how his knee responds and if he'll be available against the Hawks.

Great work from Redick to have the team ready to go from the start on Sunday. They jumped out to an early lead and forced the Pistons to play from behind all game. He has this team in a great spot mentally and schematically right now.

#2883. These states are offering extra ACA premium subsidies in 2027

Health care coverage in some states is going to get slightly more affordable in 2027.

At least 11 states are providing funding to Obamacare enrollees to reduce their monthly insurance premiums, which qualifying enrollees should see when they sign up for coverage this open enrollment season.

The move follows a lapse of enhanced federal premium subsidies in 2025 that drove up costs for millions of people who buy health plans on the Affordable Care Act marketplace. The states include California, Colorado, Connecticut, Maryland, Massachusetts, New Mexico, New Jersey, Rhode Island, Vermont, Virginia and Washington state.

Most of the states are continuing policies they put in place for 2026, in the aftermath of the loss of the federal subsidies. But Rhode Island and Virginia are debuting their respective programs for 2027, while other states like California expanded eligibility to additional consumers.

Health policy experts said the state funding could help prevent more consumers from dropping their insurance coverage, following a significant drop decline this year. It also comes amid a broader affordability crunch for U.S. households, which voters cited as a top issue in the recent midterm elections.

While enhanced federal premium subsidies lapsed at the end of 2025, there's still a tranche of federal subsidies — known as premium tax credits — that remain in place. The funding offered by these 11 states comes on top of any federal subsidies consumers may receive.

The amount of financial assistance varies by state, and each state has different eligibility criteria. Many target their programs at lower-income consumers, experts said.

"This was an effort to make coverage more affordable again" for some people, said Louise Norris, a health policy analyst for insurance referral site healthinsurance.org. "It can certainly be significant if you qualify for the help your state provides."

The states offering additional premium assistance operate state-based health insurance marketplaces rather than HealthCare.gov. They're also largely Democratic-leaning states, health policy experts said.

Most offered premium assistance to state residents in 2026. However, Rhode Island and Virginia are new to the list for 2027.

In many of the states, officials said the move to offer state-funded premium assistance is a direct response to the lapse of enhanced ACA premium subsidies at the end of 2025.

After the lapse of the enhanced subsidies — which had been in effect since 2021 and helped push ACA enrollment to record highs — Obamacare premiums increased by an average of 58%, to $178 per month on average in 2026 from $113 per month the prior year, according to KFF, a nonpartisan health policy research group.

Democrats pushed to renew the enhanced premium subsidies — the crux of a government shutdown in 2025 that lasted more than a month — but the Republican-led Congress opted not to extend them.

ACA enrollment fell to 19.2 million as of February 2026, a 13% decline from 22.1 million in February 2025, according to figures published in June by the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services.

Evidence suggests enrollment has fallen further since then, according to a KFF analysis.

Health policy experts largely attribute the decline to the expiration of enhanced subsidies, since the lapse pushed up costs for consumers.

"Where Congress has failed, Colorado is stepping up," Gov. Jared Polis said in September in a press release discussing insurance premium assistance. "Despite the loss of the enhanced premium tax credits, we are doing everything we can to keep health insurance prices down so Coloradans can afford access to health care."

Not all consumers enrolled in an ACA marketplace health plan will be eligible for their state's financial assistance.

Of all the states, New Mexico is the only one that is fully replacing the enhanced federal premium subsidies for ACA enrollees in the state in 2027, extending a policy in place for 2026, according to health policy experts.

While ACA enrollment declined nationally in 2026, New Mexico was the only state to see an increase in its enrollment figures, growing 14% between 2025 and 2026, according to KFF.

Some states allocated more funding to their premium assistance programs for 2027 relative to 2026.

In California, for example, Gov. Gavin Newsom, a Democrat, and the California Legislature increased the amount of state funds available for the Covered California State Subsidy Program to $300 million in 2027 from $190 million in 2026, according to a press release on the state's health insurance marketplace.

Californians with incomes up to 200% of the federal poverty level in 2027 — $31,920 for an individual or $66,000 for a family of four — will be eligible for a state premium subsidy. In 2026, those subsidies are available for households with incomes up to 165% of the federal poverty line.

As a result of the eligibility expansion, an additional 200,000 will be eligible for financial help who weren't eligible in 2026, Covered California, the state marketplace, said in a press release.

Massachusetts also increased its investment in financial assistance for ACA enrollees to $620 million in 2027 from $600 million this year — the largest state investment in the country, state officials said in October.

The value of state assistance can be modest for some consumers, depending on factors like their income and monthly premiums, health policy experts said.

For example, in Washington state, ACA enrollees with an income up to 200% of the federal poverty line and who receive a federal subsidy can get a state-funded subsidy of up to $35 per month. Those with an income between 201% and 250% of the poverty line can get up to $20 per month.

That said, states that offer premium subsidies saw a lower decline in ACA marketplace enrollment than in states that didn't offer any financial assistance, experts said.

"We saw that states in 2026 that did offer assistance had smaller declines in enrollment," said Justin Lo, a senior researcher at KFF who specializes in the Affordable Care Act. "If the previous pattern continues, we will also see possibly better enrollment retention in states that are more generous with their subsidies."

It's not a guarantee states will be able to continue funding these subsidies into the future, experts said.

"Funding is obviously the crux of the issue," said Norris, of healthinsurance.org. "Whether states will be able to continue to provide funding for these programs really depends on these states' budgets."

#2882. Ravens news: Giants inquired about Justice Hill

Welcome back to Loose Feathers, a regular round-up of the latest news and analysis of the Baltimore Ravens. In today's edition, we cover trade rumors about the team's running backs, the case for Eric DeCosta to sell at the deadline, and an assessment of first-year offensive coordinator Declan Doyle, Let's dive in:

Let's be honest: the Ravens were never going to engage in a fire sale, especially not with some hope about the potential return of Lamar Jackson. Trade talk about players like Derrick Henry is silly. But moving veterans on expiring contracts who may not be re-signed or net a valuable compensatory pick in the offseason could make sense.

Baltimore's No. 2 running back, Justice Hill, fits that bill. He has been an effective third-down back and could be worth retaining on a cheap extension, but investing in two veteran running backs is the kind of resource allocation mistake DeCosta has to avoid moving forward.

Hill seems unlikely to net much, until you consider his rumored suitor: the Giants – John Harbaugh's new team, aka Ravens North. He threw all sorts of money at his players in free agency and has demonstrated a hands-on approach to the deadline. Getting a friendly deal for a solid Day 3 pick from his longtime friend could have been a tidy piece of business for DeCosta, but the team's win-now mindset will likely keep Hill in Baltimore.

In the event a trade received consideration, the Giants and Patriots represented logical landing spots. Henry began his career under Mike Vrabel, who is now in New England. During the 2024 and '25 campaigns, meanwhile, he played under John Harbaugh. Harbaugh's Giants are known to be in the market for a running back addition, and Schefter reports the team called about Ravens backup Justice Hill.

Hill has spent his entire eight-year career in Baltimore, making him a highly familiar face for Harbaugh. The former fourth-rounder has carved out a role as Baltimore's top pass-catching back over time, and his work in pass protection and on special teams add to his value. Hill is attached to a salary of $2.75MM in 2026 and is a pending free agent.

The Ravens are trading for quarterback Jacoby Brissett from the Arizona Cardinals in an effort to save a season that has been derailed by injuries. That means they probably won't be as active as sellers as they could have been before that trade. They could still look to move some players who won't be around in 2027. Cornerback Marlon Humphrey has earned a 64.7 PFF coverage grade this season and is a candidate to be shipped out. His contract isn't the most team-friendly for the acquiring team, but if the Ravens could get a late-round pick for him, this could make sense.

Pick 1Ted selects: Declan Doyle, Baltimore Ravens OC

The 30-year-old fits the mold of the most in-demand coaches: a hot young play caller from a strong coaching tree. Doyle could be the first branch of the Ben Johnson tree, and you can see the shared DNA in his system with the Ravens. When Lamar Jackson was healthy, we saw a glimpse of how explosive this offense could be, as Doyle got Jackson to play under center more often. Production is hard when you're down to your third-string QB — and the Ravens agreed to trade for Jacoby Brissett on Wednesday — but Doyle's creativity and ability to put players in position to emphasize their obvious strengths, albeit in a short sample size. — Ted Nguyen

#2881. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

There was hope that it wasn't that bad since he managed to walk off the field, but the Tide will be without him. There is no doubt that Ford will be on the sideline inspiring his teammates.

Alabama is now sitting pretty for the College Football Playoff, but they can't afford to drop a game considering what's left on the schedule.

Alabama was No. 5 in the CFP rankings heading into Baton Rouge, and with the victory, the Crimson Tide might even move up in the rankings this week.

A playoff spot is not locked up, though. Alabama can't hit cruise control from here. Alabama, with a 9-3 record that included a loss to LSU, could have made the field. But a Crimson Tide team that's 9-3 with a loss to either Vanderbilt or Auburn could easily get left out of the playoff.

So, Alabama adds a signature victory to its resume already with wins over ranked squads in Kentucky and South Carolina. The Crimson Tide must keep its foot on the pedal, though.

There is no sugarcoating that Vanderbilt is a bad football team. As far as Auburn goes, well…

Auburn can go 7-5 if they can beat Samford and a Kamario-less Mississippi State, but they were drubbed by both LSU and Ole Miss recently. Kalen DeBoer's squads have had a bit of history of playing down to opponents. Let's not, OK?

As far as the SEC Championship Game goes, Alabama's hopes are pinned to LSU, as the Tigers host Texas this week then travel to Tennessee next week. Alabama will not win a tiebreaker against either of those teams, whether a two team or otherwise. If the tie is a four-way of the current two-loss teams or a three-way between Texas, Kentucky, and Alabama, the Longhorns will go because Tennessee will be the best conference win of the bunch. Texas would best Alabama with the same criteria in a two-team tiebreaker. If the tie is Tennessee, Kentucky, and Alabama then the Vols will go because they are 2-0 in the Round Robin.

So, Alabama's only path to making it to Atlanta is winning out and having LSU knock off both Texas and Tennessee. That seems quite unlikely.

"You guys were chasing him like he was Nick Saban. They've already got three losses, man," Van Pelt said.

Despite making $11.5 million as the sixth-highest-paid coach in college football, Riley remains stuck on the playoff bubble. And how in the world is Kiffin convincing Hollywood elite like Kevin Costner to usher him into the stadium when Riley is posted up in LA? Maybe The Postman mistakenly thought the "LA" abbreviation referred to Kiffin's old Los Angeles address.

Texas A&M's Mike Elko is not far behind at $10.8 million, ranking ninth. His Aggies have five losses, big production costs and terrible box office returns.

Kiffin's entire career has been all flash. At some point, being handed the nation's most talented roster will result in a postseason run for Kiffin, but the slow burn to that marker — and his defection from Ole Miss and previous late-season meltdowns — continue to be a troubling trend for the Bayou Barker.

LSU's players have taken on the class of their head coach.

#2878. CoreWeave confronts 'doom cycle' narrative as neocloud credit risk overshadows growth

After a tumultuous month for neoclouds that saw stock prices cut in half, investors are eager to hear how the leading companies in the space plan to navigate a tightening credit market and an unfavorable political environment.

CoreWeave is set to report quarterly results on Tuesday, followed by Nebius on Thursday. Both companies are reliant on debt to fund their purchases of cutting-edge artificial intelligence systems, but they're confronting a skeptical base of lenders as the AI trade sours on Wall Street.

The neoclouds had a particularly rough day last Thursday, suffering dramatic drops and contributing to the Nasdaq's steepest slump in 19 months. Leading chipmakers Nvidia, Advanced Micro Devices and Micron also plummeted on concern that raging anti-AI sentiment would further complicate buildout plans.

Nvidia, a major investor in a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave and Nebius, faces its own fresh turbulence after CEO Jensen Huang admitted Sunday that he'd had an extramarital affair with a business executive who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy. That news could add further volatility to an already nervous market.

Democrats last week won governors' races in Georgia, Iowa, Nevada, Ohio and Texas, and voters rejected data centers in all but one of the 41 related measures on ballots across the country.

CoreWeave CEO Mike Intrator took to LinkedIn to try to temper investors' fears.

"We currently don't anticipate any impact to CoreWeave's existing customer commitments or associated backlog as a result of shifts in the political landscape," Intrator wrote.

But the market was unrelenting. Yields on some CoreWeave bonds maturing in 2031 jumped above 16%. The spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, soared above 1,240 basis points, up 50% in the past month.

"I do agree completely that neoclouds in particular are going to be the most impacted," Anastasia Amoroso, chief investment strategist at Partners Group, told CNBC's "Closing Bell" on Friday. Unlike hyperscalers Amazon and Microsoft, the top neoclouds are unprofitable and heavily dependent on a small number of customers.

IREN, a smaller publicly traded neocloud, said in its earnings report late Thursday that about 48% of its backlog was tied to Microsoft. But the company provided some relief to the market after reporting that AI cloud revenue more than doubled from the prior period. IREN's stock jumped 20% on Friday, recouping its losses from Thursday.

Daniel Roberts, co-CEO of IREN, told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level," but he acknowledged the challenge posed by financial markets.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call. His company currently has $10 billion in debt.

At the end of June, CoreWeave was sitting on $5.5 billion in cash and $35 billion in debt. The company owed $1.3 billion at a 15% interest rate on a facility that comes due in March 2028, and had $3.2 billion in obligations on an August 2030 facility at an 11% rate, according to the latest earnings statement.

CoreWeave is unlikely to go bankrupt in the near future, and it has the funds to deliver on existing contracts, but growth over the next five years will require more capital, said Luke Yang, a Morningstar analyst covering CoreWeave.

"If they want to issue money through equities, the stock price is not working in their favor, because the stock has been beaten down," Yang said. "So I also want to see how management talks about how to close this financing gap."

Yang said he's also paying attention to the broader neocloud market.

Nscale, based in the U.K., filed paperwork for an IPO in September but hasn't published an updated prospectus since then. "I wouldn't say it's a good time for them to go public," Yang said.

SB Energy, which filed at the beginning of September, is in a similar predicament, and Australian neocloud Firmus nixed plans for an IPO last month.

The uncertainty has not helped CoreWeave's case, Intrator told employees in an October memo seen by CNBC."But these companies are trying to raise enormous amounts of capital without the execution, revenue, scale or software solutions that CoreWeave delivers to its customers," he wrote.Investors are still on watch. Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets, told CNBC's "Power Lunch" on Thursday that the narrative is "just so negative."

"Unfortunately, it's a self-fulfilling prophecy, a doom cycle, where if the creditors are worried about CoreWeave paying back their debts, then of course they have to borrow going forward to fund their AI infrastructure build at exorbitant rates," he said.

CoreWeave generated $5 billion in revenue in 2025. Meeks estimates that number jumping to $42 billion in 2028, with an EBITDA (earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization) margin that year of close to 70%.

"If they can get on that path and folks feel comfortable with that path, then they'll feel more comfortable about the debts and CoreWeave's financial wherewithal to pay those debts."

For the most recent quarter, analysts expect revenue growth of about 160% from a year earlier to $3.55 billion, according to LSEG. Nebius is expected to report sixfold growth to about $908 million.

Meeks said he expects the market to get "some relief" from both earnings reports.

CoreWeave declined to comment. Nebius didn't respond to a request for comment.

For Meeks, the key is getting capital markets back on board with the neoclouds' growth story, and believing that profits are on the way.

"You can have a healthy bank, but if there's a run on banks generally speaking then they could be in some trouble," he said. "So I am not fool enough to downplay the credit risk."
